Proponents correctly maintain that individual differences do not invariably defeat a common monitoring program. That proposition does not cure these demonstrated entitlement problems. Nor would an unexplained appeal to predominance or management difficulty suffice to deny a different supported (b)(2) proposal. The Court adopts no additional universal indivisibility requirement beyond the actual rule, decides no new medical fact or Pennsylvania claim element, and does not design a replacement monitoring program.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Medical-monitoring classes are not categorically barred, and tobacco deception receives no immunity. Rule 23(a)'s requirements remain applicable, but the Court makes no independent adequacy-conflict holding drawn from Amchem's different settlement facts. It decides no separate named claim, individual standing question, new limitations rule or medical-necessity rule. The class ruling creates no assumed jurisdiction over individual substantive merits. A different proposal would require an available procedural route, appropriate actual representatives and satisfaction of every applicable prerequisite; neither its availability nor its result is decided.

#### Precedent Treatment

- General Telephone Co. of the Southwest v. Falcon supplies rigorous attention to the actual Rule 23 prerequisites; the Court applies that inquiry without turning certification into a prediction of ultimate merits success.
- Castano v. American Tobacco Co., decided January 14, 1997, supplies the actual governing-law and proof inquiry and preserves its rejection of categorical novelty or size barriers. Its (b)(3) predominance and superiority rules remain confined to their proper setting; its unappealed (b)(2) denial did not decide this medical-monitoring question.
- Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, decided June 25, 1997, supplies the requirement to satisfy applicable Rule 23 conditions and preserve lawful representation. Its settlement-specific conflicts and (b)(3) predominance application are not adopted as Barnes facts or requirements. Its limited antecedent nonmerits sequencing does not permit assumed jurisdiction to decide individual substantive claims.
- In re Rhone-Poulenc Rorer, decided February 26, 1997, preserves lawful substantive defenses. The Court does not extend its distinct jury constraints into a new Seventh Amendment holding about this proposed equitable relief.

#### Law After Decision

Courts must connect the differences among proposed monitoring-class members to the actual Rule 23(a) or (b)(2) requirement at issue. This proposal fails (b)(2)'s classwide-relief requirement because the stated individual issues affect entitlement and defenses. Individual differences, medical monitoring, class size and the equitable label are not categorical substitutes for that inquiry. Rule 23(b)(3)'s distinct requirements and the substantive law governing the named claims remain unchanged. No precedent is overruled.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/barnes_v_american_tobacco_co_merits_1999-04-07.md).

## Court, participation, and institutional practice

### Court and seniority

All nine seats are occupied at the opening of OT1998. The Supreme Court Composition register controls the roster, service dates and seniority. All nine Justices are seated before OT1998 opens, subject to established case-specific nonparticipation; no vacancy or new oath-date participation condition applies.

| Seniority | Justice | Office | Seat | Supreme Court service began | Current office began | Source and effective date |
|---:|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Alex-Lamar Stone-Zsela | Chief Justice | Chief Justice line | October 7, 1991 | October 7, 1991 | Composition register; opening substitution effective October 7, 1991. |
| 2 | John Paul Stevens | Senior Associate Justice | Stevens line | December 19, 1975 | December 19, 1975 | Composition register; seated at the OT1998 opening. |
| 3 | Sandra Day O'Connor | Associate Justice | O'Connor line | September 25, 1981 | September 25, 1981 | Composition register; seated at the OT1998 opening. |
| 4 | Antonin Scalia | Associate Justice | Scalia line | September 26, 1986 | September 26, 1986 | Composition register; seated at the OT1998 opening. |
| 5 | Anthony M. Kennedy | Associate Justice | Kennedy line | February 18, 1988 | February 18, 1988 | Composition register; seated at the OT1998 opening. |
| 6 | David H. Souter | Associate Justice | Souter line | October 9, 1990 | October 9, 1990 | Composition register; seated at the OT1998 opening. |
| 7 | Clarence Thomas | Associate Justice | Marshall line | October 23, 1991 | October 23, 1991 | Composition register; seated at the OT1998 opening. |
| 8 | Ruth Bader Ginsburg | Associate Justice | White line | August 10, 1993 | August 10, 1993 | Composition register; seated at the OT1998 opening. |
| 9 | Stephen G. Breyer | Most-junior Associate Justice | Blackmun line | August 3, 1994 | August 3, 1994 | Composition register; took seat August 3, 1994. |

**Opinion-assignment seniority:** For each judgment component, the Chief Justice assigns when he is in the judgment majority. Otherwise the most senior participating Associate Justice in that majority assigns, in this order: Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.

### Circuit allotments

The order effective August 3, 1994 governs at the OT1998 opening.

| Eleventh Circuit | Clarence Thomas, Associate Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |
| Federal Circuit | Alex-Lamar Stone-Zsela, Chief Justice | August 3, 1994 | August 3 allotment order. |

**Allotment rule:** A membership change triggers a complete reallotment. No Justice inherits a predecessor's circuits. Under the Composition register, Stone reassesses the entire map for institutional effectiveness; an express administrative order may also change an allotment.

### Standing practice

**Referral of applications, effective October 7, 1991; signed explanations and certification, effective October 2, 1995.** By the Court's internal practice adopted at the opening of October Term 1991, an application for a stay, injunction, or other interim relief in a matter on the Term's inventory is referred by the Circuit Justice to the full Court and decided by the participating Justices under the participating-majority rule. The order issues in the Court's name and recites the presenting Circuit Justice ("presented to Justice ___ and by him or her referred to the Court"). From October 2, 1995, referral does not prevent the Circuit Justice, or the substitute the term-effective Rules designate, from taking a necessary temporary action in his or her own name while the Court considers the application. Each action is explained in short signed writings, including the Circuit Justice's own explanation at final disposition, whether prevailing or dissenting, and a statement by each participating Justice in each Court action deciding relief, and is certified for issuance by the Chief Justice or the Chief's substitute, as Engine §10 provides. A Justice may participate in a referred application from the date of the judicial oath. An application not on the Term's inventory remains subject to ordinary Circuit Justice practice.

No additional case-specific nonparticipation is established by the opening setting. The fact that all nine seats are occupied does not establish participation in a particular event; that depends on the supported argument or submission and decision facts.

Vey v. Clinton's optional Rule 38 payment and Rule 33.1 compliance opportunity ran through June 30, 1997. The two Arteaga petitions remain distinct; their permitted payment-and-form opportunity ran through March 16, 1998. Glendora's permitted opportunity ran through March 30, 1998. Passage of those dates does not establish compliance, noncompliance, default, or a terminal Court action. Any further event must establish the relevant procedure from supported facts or an expressly authorized bounded premise.

The four Breard-related emergency applications were denied on April 14, 1998. No continuing stay, April 16 return, or execution is inferred. The four companion petitions or leave requests listed above remain pending independently. Neither an application denial nor missing research material decides those separate matters.

Lower-court remands from completed decisions do not create additional Supreme Court events. Alaska and New Jersey retain their specifically recorded decree stages; Bryan County retains its reargument stage. New Jersey's March 31 proposal and May 30 Designated Survey remain proposals, not a final decree or operative undefined injunction. No date or filing for an unscheduled continuation is invented.


### Completed chunk 2 mandates

### Wright v. Universal Maritime Service Corp. — 1998-11-16

The action returns through the Fourth Circuit to the District Court without the unsupported grievance-exhaustion dismissal. The dismissal was without prejudice, and the ADA claim and independent defenses remain for ordinary proceedings. No damages, reinstatement, disability-benefit estoppel, grievance or arbitration is ordered. No further Supreme Court event is set.

[Public mandate](../entering-law/wright_v_universal_maritime_service_corp_merits_1998-11-16.md#procedure-after-action).

### Dodd v. Hood River County — 1998-11-19

The Ninth Circuit judgment stands on the confined preclusion ruling and independently stated federal grounds. Supreme Court review is complete. No compensation, new permit, additional trial or further factfinding is ordered, and no further Supreme Court event is set.

[Public mandate](../entering-law/dodd_v_hood_river_county_merits_1998-11-19.md#procedure-after-action).

### Wisconsin Right to Life, Inc. v. Paradise — 1998-11-24

The case returns to the Seventh Circuit for consideration of the actual claims and requested relief under the stated Article III requirements, and appropriate treatment of the District Court's abstention disposition thereafter. No injunction, statutory invalidation, state-registration command or direction governing an unpresented state case issues. Further proceedings below remain open; no additional Supreme Court proceeding or deadline is set.

[Public mandate](../entering-law/wisconsin_right_to_life_v_paradise_merits_1998-11-24.md#procedure-after-action).

### AT&T Universal Card Services v. Rembert — 1998-12-01

The Sixth Circuit judgment concerning AT&T's presented debt remains in force. No additional evidentiary hearing, remand, amount determination or fee award is ordered. Supreme Court review of this matter is complete, and no further event is set.

[Public mandate](../entering-law/att_universal_card_services_v_rembert_merits_1998-12-01.md#procedure-after-action).

### Minnesota v. Carter and Minnesota v. Johns — 1998-12-01

The cases return to the Minnesota Supreme Court for ordinary lawful further proceedings on the reviewed federal suppression claims. The adopted personal-interest rule governs both respondents. The mandate permits lawful consideration of observation and remaining properly available grounds without a Court search holding; it does not declare the record insufficient or require renewed analysis as a controlling legal conclusion.

Trial findings, reproduced testimony, appellate characterization and party assertions retain their distinct roles. If a further disposition depends on rejecting an appellate factual characterization, the governing factual-review and preservation objections must be addressed; an answer that instead assumes the disputed additions must explain why its conclusion follows on that assumption. No new hearing, photographic reconstruction, discovery, public-access burden or factual finding is ordered. The subjective-expectation objection is not adjudicated because subjective privacy is assumed. No binding curtilage concession, criminal-purpose forfeiture, state privacy offense or independent state-law judgment is found. A genuinely available independent state ground remains a matter for lawful state proceedings, not an outcome directed here.

The stop, gun discovery, arrests, warrants, physical evidence and statements retain their actual separate issues. The State's lack of probable cause before observation and the prosecutor's conditional invalid-arrest concession are not converted into an all-evidence order. Later warrants are not presumed independent from a viewing the trial court treated as central to their basis. Ordinary preservation rules control any remaining claim, and no waived or unpresented issue is revived automatically. No admission, exclusion, acquittal, new trial, release or unconditional conviction reinstatement is ordered. No further Supreme Court event or retained jurisdiction is set.

[Public mandate](../entering-law/minnesota_v_carter_and_johns_merits_1998-12-01.md#procedure-after-action).

### Equality Foundation of Greater Cincinnati, Inc. v. City of Cincinnati — 1998-12-03

The matter returns through the Sixth Circuit for declaratory and injunctive protection against the unconstitutional charter barrier, conformity of the injunction to the violation and applicable severability, and remaining lawful §1988 review. The rational-basis merits are resolved. No new code, damages, fixed fee award or additional Supreme Court event is ordered.

[Public mandate](../entering-law/equality_foundation_v_cincinnati_merits_1998-12-03.md#procedure-after-action).

The case returns to the Iowa courts for proceedings under the corrected search rule and ordinary properly available remedial law. No acquittal, release, replacement sentence, civil remedy or additional Supreme Court event is directed.

[Public mandate](../entering-law/knowles_v_iowa_merits_1998-12-08.md#procedure-after-action).

## Prospective events and material dependencies

Chunk 5 records 13 completed Court events. Germany's original leave request remains under consideration, without a further date. Stewart's application and summary review are complete, following its separate grant. Gable's Supreme Court review is complete on the continuing-controversy remand; its constitutional merits remain undecided.

Germany's retained original leave request supplies no final original-jurisdiction or treaty-merits holding. A supported dated continuation remains necessary for that pending component. The completed Stewart and Gable decisions supply only the holdings and procedural consequences stated below.


All twelve chunk 4 Court matters are completed on their assigned dates. The December 23 Maine qualification remains an independently admitted administrative event; Hart's February 11 judgment separately resolves federal mootness and the treatment of the state judgment. No Supreme Court event remains pending from chunk 4.


Use the [manifest](manifest.md) for all remaining scheduled and carried matters. A scheduled date supplies no filing, argument, vote or disposition. Same-day independent matters use the common entering-law baseline; Section 8 assignments follow alphabetical case order on that date. Later historical actions never enter by scheduling alone. Del Monte Dunes remains a reconciliation-only item against its completed June 16, 1997 judgment. The four Breard-related applications were denied; their separate petitions remain as listed above. No execution or new stay is inferred.

NAACP, Los Angeles Branch v. Jones, No. 97-1840, completed Supreme Court review on November 5, 1998. Its lower-court standing remand creates no additional Supreme Court event or scheduled return. The next scheduled event after the chronology cursor is In re Sealed Case, No. 98-93, on 1999-04-13, in OT_1998CHUNK7. Open and stopped earlier matters remain pending on their stated conditions; the schedule supplies no prospective holding.

### Reno v. American-Arab Anti-Discrimination Committee; No. 97-1252; Ninth Circuit judgment, 119 F.3d 1367. — 1999-02-24

Supreme Court merits review is complete. The case returns through the Ninth Circuit to the District Court for the confined reconsideration in Part IV. The two existing district-court orders remain effective provisionally within their existing, properly construed scope until lower-court action; the appellate court's unqualified preservation of relief does not bind that reconsideration.

The lower courts must address the Six's actual dissolution posture, the Two's own preliminary showing, legal and status changes, each person's live controversy, the supported ongoing injury and whether stopping proceedings is necessary or narrower protection suffices. They may use the developed record without a compelled new inquiry and must modify or dissolve individual relief when its requirements are absent. Barakat and Sharif receive no adjudicated protection against hypothetical new charges. No permanent injunction, new discovery entitlement, mandatory hearing, fixed deadline, removal direction or retained Supreme Court proceeding is created. Ordinary mandate procedure applies.

[Disposition and participation](../entering-law/reno_v_american_arab_anti_discrimination_committee_merits_1999-02-24.md).

The separate fee issue is outside review; the reported settlement's unrecovered terms are not construed. No Supreme Court filing deadline is created.

### Cedar Rapids Community School District v. Garret F.; No. 96-1793; OT1998. — 1999-03-03

The affirmed 1993-1994 reimbursement remains in effect. The district must include the necessary school-day nursing in the IEP, subject to lawful changes in Garret's needs and placement and lawful qualified staffing. No additional sum or employment form is prescribed. The Court reaches no independent Iowa-law ruling and directs no further Supreme Court proceeding.

### Federal Republic of Germany, Chrobog and Rudolph v. United States and Hull; No. 127, Original; A-736. — 1999-03-03

The present restraint requests are denied. Original leave remains pending for further consideration, without a fixed response or decision deadline. The proposed March 5 endpoint in the dissent creates no operative restraint or expiration. The order neither commands execution nor changes the separate Stewart application or any other restraint.

### National Federation of Federal Employees, Local 1309 v. Department of the Interior / Federal Labor Relations Authority v. Department of the Interior; Nos. 97-1184 and 97-1243. — 1999-03-03

Both petitions return to the Fourth Circuit for remand to the Authority. The Authority may reach the same negotiability conclusion if it reasonably explains that choice under the governing statute, but the Court directs neither that result nor present enforcement of the existing Survey order. Bargaining does not itself require agreement or concession; the Panel's conditioned statutory authority remains subject to the chapter and no actual Panel award is decided.

The disposition supplies no retroactive bargaining effect, imposed contract term, new agency-head-disapproval claim, or renewed proceeding against Interior. The original administrative sixty-day notice-posting and thirty-day reporting periods do not restart under the Supreme Court mandate. Their procedural treatment belongs in the statutory review and remand channel; no additional stay or deadline is created.

[Disposition and participation](../entering-law/nffe_and_flra_v_interior_merits_1999-03-03.md).

### Stewart v. LaGrand; No. 98-1412; distinct State application A-735. — 1999-03-03

The separate certiorari grant preceded the same-day application and summary-merits decision. The grant itself changed no injunction and set no new briefing or argument date. Current posture is governed by the completed application and summary decision immediately below.

### Stewart v. LaGrand — application and summary merits — 1999-03-03

The State's application A-735 is granted and the Ninth Circuit's gas-specific injunction is vacated. On the previously granted petition, the appellate judgment is reversed insofar as it awards relief on the particular elected gas-method objection, and the case returns for proceedings consistent with that limited final resolution. Part II alone would permit lawful claim-specific reconsideration and independently justified new interim relief; it does not authorize relitigation of the objection resolved by Part I. No substitute restraint, execution command, certificate rescission, briefing deadline or argument date is ordered. No other order is vacated, and no execution is found to have occurred. Germany's separate original matter is unaffected.

Supreme Court review is complete. The causes return to the Sixth Circuit for the confined continuing-controversy determination and proceedings appropriate to that determination. No new trial, particular evidentiary document, merits discovery, special proof requirement, deadline or return to this Court is directed. If a controversy remains, the two claims may proceed under effective law; if not, equitable judgment treatment must account for the actual cause and timing of mootness and each petition's scope. The order supplies neither an automatic vacatur of every district-court ruling nor a command preserving every lower judgment.

## Current-term published noncontrolling positions

### United States v. Wright — 1998-10-05

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part, unjoined.** He would affirm both convictions and vacate the sentence. He proposes an arms inquiry requiring the strongest justification for a prohibition that destroys ordinary lawful armed self-defense and a substantial, demonstrable fit for other serious burdens. The inquiry asks what protected conduct is foreclosed, how severely, and whether the government's actual justification fits that burden. Public safety is weighty but cannot substitute for that examination. Dangerous uses, demonstrably dangerous persons and unusually hazardous weapon functions call for attention to the reasons for the restriction's scope. History informs protection and permissible regulation without becoming a closed catalogue. *Love* establishes the personal holder and incorporation; *Hale* and *Rybar* reject affiliation-based exclusion and military-utility immunity. Those decisions did not already adopt his proposed tier of review.

He would sustain the one machinegun count on the admitted completed .223 automatic weapon: repeated fire from one trigger function presents rapid, difficult-to-control discharge beyond ordinary semiautomatic operation. Restricting additions to civilian automatic-weapon stock directly addresses that function while leaving ordinary defensive arms available. The post-1986 scope and existing-stock exception inform the burden; military labels do not decide it. The completed weapon independently supports his count-specific affirmance, without a ruling on all disassembled receivers or conversion parts. He reads *Rybar* to require the previously missing arms analysis, which the Court may perform on an adequate record, rather than a return in every case. His affirmative disposition depends on the plea or charge independently establishing that completed-weapon basis. He adopts no universal numerical magazine limit, militia exclusion, categorical military-weapon immunity or comprehensive prohibited-arms list.

For the separate pipe-bomb count, he concludes that registering explosive destructive devices does not extinguish ordinary defensive keeping; *Hale* supports keeping registration separate. He rejects the commerce objection because removing possession would permit acquisition, retention and recirculation of prohibited stock under *Hale*, *Rybar* and *Kirk*, while preserving *Lopez*'s limit on generalized crime costs and declining a rule based merely on out-of-state manufacture. He rejects an unlimited Ninth Amendment exemption from these fitted restrictions.

On sentencing, he agrees that the quoted remarks mix sincerity with the erroneous affiliation premise and do not establish that an independent lawful finding controlled. The section 3E1.1 commentary forecloses denial solely for the reserved legal challenge. Resentencing must evaluate actual remorse, candor and conduct without treating that legal defense as factual nonacceptance. He would order no automatic adjustment and would not resolve credibility on appeal. His proposed disposition entails no acquittal, return of weapons or new trial. He does not join the Court's opinion.

### Hiley v. Barrett — 1998-10-09

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in the judgment, unjoined.** He agrees with the entire affirmance on his stated grounds without joining the Court's opinion. He preserves a distinct personal-right and classification ground. *Love* protects persons but does not make adjudicated violent conduct irrelevant. The force, attempted-force or deadly-weapon predicate within a domestic relationship, together with counsel and jury-where-entitled protections and the statute's relief provisions, connects the disability to preventing armed recurrence. A misdemeanor label and armed employment do not eliminate that connection. Hiley's particular predicate is treated as qualifying here; no new conviction is invented.

He would not rewrite retained civil rights as restored rights merely because state systems differ or some misdemeanors caused no initial rights loss. That statutory position goes beyond the Court's decision to leave the never-lost-rights construction unresolved. His personal-right discussion supplies his alternative ground for rejecting the preserved classification challenge; it does not adjudicate a separately preserved arms cause or revive procedural due process. He approves no disarmament based on any misdemeanor, an allegation or a nonviolent status, and leaves a genuinely different as-applied record open.

He retains jurisdiction because successful federal relief would restore Hiley's armed-deputy assignment, while respecting the local settlement. His reasoning preserves the identified supply-and-possession mechanism, the distinct rejection of a state-administration command, and the prospective, nonpunitive character of this restriction. He would neither issue a criminal judgment, order reinstatement, reopen settled claims nor decide a future prosecution's notice defense or a claimed breach of the settlement.

### United States v. Louisiana (Texas Boundary Case) — 1998-10-13

No separate writing issues.

### Peoples Rights Organization, Inc. v. City of Columbus — 1998-10-15

**Justice Stevens, concurring in part and dissenting in part, unjoined.** He joins the Court's threshold, fault, definition and noncity-exception analyses and its common severability principles. He would affirm invalidity of both former-city-registration selectors. His Nordlinger objection to entrenched ownership and family preferences and his Heller and Romer positions require a meaningful relation to an impartial public purpose. Lawful pre-existing owners who registered and those unable to identify the invalid old duty are alike in the ownership reliance invoked here. Owners first covered by the replacement ordinance had no meaningful opportunity to enter the only registry. For him those defects disconnect the closed registry from the claimed protection, even though actual registration occurred. A clear prospective cutoff or an opportunity for all covered prior owners would present a different case. He does not require universal grandfathering, invalidate the independent NFA or inert-object exceptions, or design a replacement scheme.

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part, unjoined.** He would affirm the Sixth Circuit's relief against all five challenged definition categories and both former-city-registration distinctions, while sustaining the remaining NFA magazine exception. Smolak and Walker's actual possession, closed registration window and threatened prosecution justify present review; P.R.O. seeks common relief for similarly situated members. No advisory judgment about a wholly hypothetical weapon is warranted.

In his view, the rifle definition does not choose among actual magazine possession, commercial availability and merely possible manufacture; the shotgun definition does not select a measuring shell length. Undefined modification and inaccessible original handgun-design history compound the handgun defect. Restoration and parts provisions inherit those uncertainties and add uncertain assembly conditions. Criminal exposure without a clear culpability safeguard makes those defects consequential for ordinary owners and grants enforcement discretion the text does not constrain. The City's conflicting suggested knowledge standard cannot rewrite the ordinance. He therefore does not adopt the Court's default-fault and definition constructions. Love reinforces intelligible notice for protected keeping but establishes neither a right to every listed weapon nor a judicially chosen capacity limit.

He rejects grandfathering based on success in guessing an invalid 1989 registration duty. That objection reaches section 2323.31(B)(3) and the matching city clause in section 2323.32(B)(2). NFA registration instead supplies an independent, intelligible reliance basis. Its broader coverage than magazines individually used with the registered firearm survives ordinary rationality under Romer without exact tailoring. He also agrees with the judgment sustaining the separate related inert-object magazine exception, adding no ground on that component. He would implement the declaratory and injunctive relief for the invalid provisions, preserve separable valid exceptions, and enact neither damages nor a fresh registry. He decides no unpresented substantive arms claim and joins no part of the Court's opinion.

### United States v. Franklyn / United States v. Gonzalez — 1998-10-22

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in the judgment, unjoined.** He agrees that the consolidated judgments should be affirmed while keeping each appellant's issues separate. Franklyn's completed automatic weapon falls within the national stock restriction: without the possession rule, prohibited new stock could be retained and circulated beyond transfer enforcement. Hale, Rybar and Kirk therefore supply the fitted mechanism, without a new interstate-travel element or a general claim that local violence affects national productivity. He supplies no Second Amendment ruling.

He regards the late strike objection, made after the venire members had been excused, as a genuine preservation problem because the ordinary opportunity for immediate correction was lost. His counsel analysis also depends on the reported alternative determination actually crediting the prosecutor's explanations in context. Hernandez permits respect for that supported ultimate finding; neutral wording alone would not suffice. On that finding, earlier objection would not probably have produced a different outcome and Strickland prejudice is unproved. If the actual transcript showed only facial neutrality, the alternative ground could not substitute for a missing ultimate determination. He preserves that condition and supplies no missing juror explanation.

For Gonzalez's sentence, jointly undertaken conduct and foreseeability must connect the machinegun and discharge to him; physical proximity alone cannot do so. The ammunition and coordinated episode supply the reported basis. Actual discharge adds danger not exhausted by the possession calculation, supporting section 5K2.6. Separately, prior weapons and drug conduct insufficiently reflected in the initial criminal-history score can support section 4A1.3 if the selected category is explained. Koon permits respect for the contextual decision while forbidding an unexplained increase or repeated punishment for an already-counted factor. He adds no factual finding, increases no sentence and adopts no automatic rule for all ammunition offenders.

He also agrees with the dispositions of Gonzalez's separate section 922(g) commerce objection and the other-trial-evidence and within-range-selection components, adding no grounds on those components. No new trial or resentencing follows from the reported record. He joins no part of the Court's opinion.

### United States v. Griffin / United States v. Langley — 1998-10-29

No separate writing issues; all nine Justices join the complete opinion.

### Marquez v. Screen Actors Guild, Inc., No. 97-1056. — 1998-11-03

No separate writing is filed. The common opinion preserves the limits concerning actual deception, notice, enforcement and statutory jurisdiction.

### Coalition for Economic Equity v. Wilson — 1998-11-10

O'Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, agrees that voluntary-action permission does not establish blanket Title VII preemption, but would affirm vacatur of the preliminary injunction. The dissent treats Proposition 209 as a statewide substantive restriction on officials' classifications, leaving ordinary antidiscrimination protection available. Crawford preserves the State's choice to withdraw optional policies; lawful remedies under Hopwood and Engineering Contractors are not compulsory policies. Hunter concerned unequal access to ordinary racial protection, and Seattle concerned specially displaced desegregation authority. In the dissent's view, those decisions do not entrench every optional preference-making power at a subordinate governmental level. The reciprocal sex provision likewise does not establish the asserted facial denial of equal protection. This position preserves scrutiny of actual unequal administration, binding federal obligations and properly presented later applications, and does not declare every affirmative measure unconstitutional.

### Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith, Inc. v. Kravitz, No. 97-1768. — 1998-11-12

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring; no joins.** The Chief Justice joins the Court's knowing-deception holding and affirmance. He would also hold that reckless, materially misleading acceptance of customer orders under the existing best-execution undertaking can constitute primary § 10(b) and Rule 10b-5 fraud when every ordinary private-action requirement is proved. Acceptance communicates an intention to honor that undertaking; an actionable false representation need not be confined to a defendant's express statement. His broader culpability position goes beyond the sufficient ground the Court adopts and does not become the Court's rule.

He agrees that best execution depends on terms reasonably available under contemporary market conditions, that industry custom informs but does not conclusively resolve culpability, and that later SEC rules cannot create retroactive liability. Later market studies may corroborate evolving feasibility without supplying a retroactive legal command. He would leave knowing or reckless falsity, reliance and actual damage for case-specific proof. Neither breach of fiduciary duty alone, execution at the national best bid and offer nor the existence of a better price automatically establishes fraud, and the concurrence recognizes no reliance presumption from price disparity.

### Dodd v. Hood River County — 1998-11-19

Stone-Zsela concurs in Part I and dissents from final resolution of the remaining federal issues. He agrees that §1738 governs the lawful effect of matters actually, necessarily and fairly decided in Oregon and that a federal reservation cannot erase the binding profitable-use finding. He also agrees that the whole federal cause and its unadjudicated elements are not precluded merely because state takings litigation occurred.

He would preserve that narrow preclusion and return the federal questions outside the state determination for adjudication. He does not join the Court's independent conclusions under Lucas, Penn Central, the purpose-relationship inquiry or the actual-period claim. His position neither finds a taking nor directs compensation, a permit or a jury trial, and it does not announce a separate substantive takings rule. The disagreement concerns final adjudication of those federal issues in this disposition after recognizing the limited state effect.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/dodd_v_hood_river_county_merits_1998-11-19.md#opinion-topology).

### Wisconsin Right to Life, Inc. v. Paradise — 1998-11-24

Scalia dissents, joined by Thomas. They accept that a credible threatened injury may support review before prosecution and that a facial invalidity claim differs from an advisory instruction about future litigation. They would nevertheless affirm dismissal because the existing official and private-enforcement facts already defeat this action. The officials agree that WRTL's comparative guides do not require registration, and no threat directed at WRTL is shown. Proceedings involving different speakers do not supply the missing official connection. Independent private plaintiffs and state judges are absent, so an injunction against the named officials cannot simply stop their actions.

The dissent distinguishes Maine's effective rule credibly reaching ceased speech and Bennett's announced governmental action affecting the plaintiffs. NAACP corrected a court's bypass of disputed standing to reach merits; it does not require another inquiry when the defendant-specific defect has already been addressed. A facial prayer cannot create that missing connection, and hoped-for persuasive effect against nonparties does not itself supply likely redress here. The dissent adopts no universal prosecution prerequisite, no rule that every facial challenge is advisory and no conclusion about Wisconsin's constitutional merits. It would affirm jurisdictional dismissal without prejudice.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/wisconsin_right_to_life_v_paradise_merits_1998-11-24.md#opinion-topology).

### Minnesota v. Carter and Minnesota v. Johns — 1998-12-01

**Breyer, Part II, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.** These four would conclusively hold that this observation was not a search. The trial findings and testimony establish an unenclosed area actually used by residents and nonresidents, including access beside the window for bicycles, and a view obtainable by natural sight without touching the window, lighting it or using equipment. The initial position near the projecting brickwork already disclosed the table and packaging; it was a window-side position, not a proven sidewalk view. The later move gave a closer view of the same activity.

They assume in respondents' favor the disputed climbing and crouching. Crossing shrubs not shown to enclose the space and adjusting posture do not, by themselves, turn the actual natural view from this ordinarily used position into a search. The approximate 12–18-inch finding is no constitutional minimum, and uncertain gap geometry, the exact crossing of one entrance walk and the asserted failure to seek rehearing are unnecessary to this answer. They do not find the disputed acts occurred or discredit the appellate account through photographs.

Fields's limited-sharing rule remains controlling: cotenant visibility alone is not publication. Their answer rests on the fuller actual-use record, including ordinary nonresident use, and the natural view. Pinson and Myers protect otherwise concealed information and do not require disregarding what this opening actually exposed. Drawn blinds, sustained proximity, the nonordinary entrance route and the disputed bodily movements are serious contrary facts, but the four regard the actual access and unenhanced view as sufficient together. Their rationale would not resolve opening or moving blinds, crossing a protected enclosure, intruding into the dwelling or using equipment to obtain otherwise unavailable information. It creates no common-yard immunity, fixed distance or time rule, or exterior-location exception.

They accept vacatur and ordinary remand as sufficient removal of the adverse judgments while publishing that conclusive view. They do not join O'Connor's reason for further actual-vantage analysis. They accept that the absence of a Court search holding leaves lawful further consideration available; they order neither automatic admission nor unconditional conviction reinstatement. Breyer also joins the Court's affirmative personal-interest holding. The other three retain the contrary privacy position expressed in O'Connor's Part A.

**O'Connor, concurring in the judgment and dissenting in part.** In Part A, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, O'Connor would reject the accepted invitation-duration-task basis for personal privacy. Fields protects actual independent control and, separately, developed recurring residential sharing. Permission for this brief compensated task does not establish either relationship in their view. Their conclusion rests on the supported combination, not a rule that illegal activity forfeits rights, that commerce is inherently unprotected, that only an owner has privacy or that an overnight visit is indispensable. A temporary rental carrying control or genuinely shared residential or social relationship could differ.

Carter's counsel's key affidavit cannot be treated as nonexistent or as an accepted control finding. A properly usable finding of authorized independent access could change the application of Fields's control branch. Its procedural status, weight and actual permission implications are not decided; no new hearing or revived waiver is ordered. Johns has no corresponding proffer. Breyer does not join this personal-interest disagreement.

In Part B, O'Connor alone would resolve the actual-view question through renewed source-accurate analysis once the Court recognizes personal privacy. Ordinary access, the standing natural view, bicycle storage, nonresident use and lack of enclosure support the lawful-view route; screening, prolonged proximity and the position outside ordinary entrance passage support the search route. Her Fields plurality and Riley qualification preserve the claimant's burden without equating limited sharing with publication. If the constitutional answer requires rejecting an appellate factual characterization, factual-review and preservation objections must actually be addressed. If the same answer follows while assuming the disputed additions, that ground can be stated instead. Ornelas and Fields do not authorize invention of historical facts. She orders no new hearing or photographic reconstruction and imposes no new public-access burden. This is her reason for the judgment, not a Court finding that the record is insufficient or renewed analysis necessary.

**Souter, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** Part A, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens and Ginsburg, would affirm both reviewed judgments. Each respondent has his own interest in the deliberately shared domestic privacy. An officer conducts a search by deliberately obtaining screened home-interior information from a residential window-side vantage outside ordinary public observation. The close, sustained examination through drawn blinds from the nonordinary entrance approach satisfies that rule on the trial-level minimum. No finding of climbing, crouching, a state offense or a universal shared-yard curtilage classification is required.

The grass's common uses establish access, not ordinary exposure of this screened interior to the precise examination. The abutment position already required the close window-side approach; neither it nor the informant's unexplained outside view supplies a lawful sidewalk source. Fields rejects equating limited residential sharing or imperfect blinds with publication. Katz protects privacy without physical penetration. Pinson and Myers explain the protection of concealed home information, but their technological holdings did not already decide this naked-eye observation. The contrary actual-use and natural-visibility argument is substantial; these Justices find it insufficient to convert this inspection into ordinary public observation.

The conclusion preserves unaided observation from a street, walkway, common approach, open window or other position genuinely available to ordinary public observation. It requires no fence or posted sign whenever the actual residential vantage is otherwise shown not to be such a position. Neither approximately fifteen minutes nor approximately a foot is a fixed constitutional threshold. The disputed appellate additions are unnecessary to the conclusion, so this ground does not decide the factual-correction and rehearing disputes.

In Part B, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg additionally conclude that no warrant, pre-view probable cause, consent or supported exception justified the search. An investigatory tip and modest asserted intrusion supply no reasonable-suspicion exception for protected home information. Stone-Zsela does not join that additional rationale. All four would leave the actual state suppression judgments in force, subject to properly available independent issues; none orders acquittal or a new blanket exclusion of every later item. The three Associates additionally join the Court's conditional remedial rules. The separate writing does not turn the prosecutor's invalid-arrest condition into unconditional exclusion.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/minnesota_v_carter_and_johns_merits_1998-12-01.md#opinion-topology).

### Equality Foundation of Greater Cincinnati, Inc. v. City of Cincinnati — 1998-12-03

Scalia dissents, joined by Thomas. They would affirm the merits judgment, dissolution of the injunction and dependent fee vacatur. They accept that Article XII reaches ordinary nondiscrimination protection and that municipalities remain subject to equal protection. In their view, regulatory restraint, limited resources, private association and the electorate's authority to make a local policy durable rationally support the measure despite imperfect coverage. Higher-level and generally applicable remedies remain available, and a local body's control over its own delegated policies differs from Colorado's restriction of state and local channels.

The dissent maintains its disagreement with Romer's application of ordinary rationality and proposes the municipal distinction as an additional limit. It does not claim Romer's majority adopted that reasoning or that every affected protection is a quota. O'Connor's Coalition writing supplies support for withdrawal of optional policies, while Hurley preserves actual expression; neither is treated as making every orientation protection unlawful. The dissent rejects a new racial political-process extension and finds no duty to adopt or preserve these municipal protections. Its fee position follows its merits conclusion only and supplies no separate bar to a fee award resting on an independently established surviving basis.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/equality_foundation_v_cincinnati_merits_1998-12-03.md#opinion-topology).

### Knowles v. Iowa — 1998-12-08

Stone-Zsela joins the constitutional holding and the reversal but would make the remedy explicit: the Iowa courts should suppress the fruits of this citation-only search and conduct further proceedings on the remaining admissible record. The search followed no custody, found no evidence needed to preserve the speeding observation and rested on no particularized protective or other independent ground. On that established scope, he would not leave treatment of its fruits to a general remand formulation.

His concurrence concerns this search's fruits, not a universal remedy for every traffic encounter. He preserves lawful exit directions, supported Terry protection and independent probable-cause searches. He would not order acquittal, assumes no civil liability and supplies no new holding limiting searches after actual lawful custodial arrest.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/knowles_v_iowa_merits_1998-12-08.md#opinion-topology).

### Calderon v. Coleman — 1998-12-14

**Kennedy, concurring in part and in the judgment.** He would use demanding whole-record substantial-influence review in this category, with genuine grave doubt preventing a finding of harmlessness. His Brecht and O'Neal positions distinguish collateral review while requiring the State to explain why an established violation did not substantially influence the actual decision. That is neither a sufficiency-of-aggravation test nor a rule that the prisoner loses whenever the evidence is evenly balanced. He accepts the binding Chapman categories but would not extend them here. Stringer's distinct weighing-error rule does not make every capital habeas claim identical. The District Court's plausible account of diminished mitigation must be assessed against the full charge and independent institutional-danger case; he supports a return without finding harmlessness or directing denial of relief.

**Thomas, joined by Scalia, concurring in part and in the judgment.** They would require the prisoner to establish substantial injurious influence for this category outside the previously controlling Chapman holdings. Their O'Neal position rests on the limited collateral remedy and §2254(a)'s custody-in-violation requirement, while recognizing that the statute does not expressly state a tie-breaking rule. An evenly balanced showing does not satisfy their proposed burden. They respect Roy's particular personal-intent rule without extending it universally. Potential diversion and instructional misunderstanding do not themselves establish the substantial effect they require. Their objection concerns the unfinished substantive inquiry, not missing words; serious aggravation is not an automatic cure. They rely neither on New's excluded account nor on a universal innocence prerequisite, and they do not require factor-specific capital findings derived from Scalia's different omitted-purpose analysis in Roy.

**Stevens, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He would affirm under substantial-influence review with grave doubt preventing harmlessness. The District Court did more than identify inaccurate words: it connected the misleading nondeath alternative to mitigation explaining violent behavior, the prominence of the prior convictions and the prosecution's danger case. The jury's conviction-document request shows salience without proving an actual release discussion. He considers the corrective charge, repeated instructions, extensive violence, prison-safety argument and reported short deliberation, but finds that those matters do not dispel grave doubt about substantial influence. The broader danger language and the instruction itself leave a concrete connection to the sentencing choice even without an express prosecutorial reference to commutation. His Roy position permits affirmance when a proper substantive application already supports relief despite an overbroad lower formulation. Another remand would repeat that task. He relies on the trial record, not excluded deliberation evidence or mitigation developed later in habeas, and would retain sentence-only relief with a reasonable state sentencing opportunity.

**Ginsburg, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** She joins the Chapman rule for this defined capital-selection error but concludes that the State cannot establish noncontribution beyond a reasonable doubt on the existing record. The misleading account could turn explanations of violent behavior into reasons to doubt the nondeath option; the convictions emphasized in aggravation also restricted commutation. She considers the corrective direction and the substantial independent institutional-danger case, but neither establishes that the misinformation made no contribution. The charge first purported to explain life without parole through inaccurate information and then directed the jury to disregard that subject. The District Court's contextual explanation of that conflict supports its judgment. Her conclusion does not depend on a universal rule against corrective instructions, a specific parole question, New's excluded proffer or later habeas mitigation. Her O'Neal reservation of a universal Chapman extension remains, and her Roy position does not require express findings on every capital factor. She would affirm conditional sentence relief rather than require another application of the same substantive inquiry.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/calderon_v_coleman_summary_merits_1998-12-14.md#opinion-topology).

### Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc. — 1999-01-12

**Thomas, concurring in the judgment.** Thomas agrees that every reviewed restriction is invalid but applies traditional strict scrutiny to the direct speech and severe associational burdens. Registration reduces the Meyer-protected speaker pool without a narrowly fitted traceability justification. The personal-name badge directly intrudes on core political speech and fails narrow tailoring even assuming a compelling anti-fraud interest. The reports' effect on circulation is more attenuated; compelled disclosure independently burdens association and belief. Singling out paid circulators poorly fits general identification, and the need to expose each individual's identity and compensation has not been demonstrated. Monthly exposure is considered separately from final reporting. As an alternative, the challenged packages also fail Buckley's more forgiving substantial-relationship test. He does not displace Buckley's surviving disclosure holdings, adopt the Court's entire method, invalidate retained reports or decide reserved fields.

**O'Connor, joined by Breyer, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** They join Part II's complete personal-name-badge explanation: direct intrusion into political conversation requires narrow tailoring, and the retained affidavit and enforcement tools defeat this demand on this record. They would sustain registration and the challenged final and monthly reports. Under Anderson–Burdick, readily available enrollment provides an administrable connection to the formal petition process without the severe burden found by the Court; Meyer does not make every qualification equivalent to a ban on paid circulators. Reports are indirect financial-disclosure burdens rather than badges worn during persuasion. Identifying payees and individual disbursements can permit checking campaign activity beyond aggregate sums, and contemporaneous monthly information can expose abuse before the vote. They find the relationship sufficient even under more demanding disclosure review. They acknowledge the excluded unregistered speakers and possible exposure costs, but reach different applications. They propose no automatic admission rule, universal minimum scrutiny or independent disposition of reserved fields.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/buckley_v_american_constitutional_law_foundation_merits_1999-01-12.md#opinion-topology).

### El Al Israel Airlines, Ltd. v. Tsui Yuan Tseng — 1999-01-12

**Justice Stevens, dissenting.** Stevens reads Article 24's reference to Article 17 as covering injuries arising from an accident. He agrees that an accident causing only psychic injury remains governed by the Convention and cannot support a separate local recovery. He disagrees that the same displacement follows for a nonaccident: the text does not clearly extinguish local remedies for that residual category. Saks separates the event from the injury, Floyd leaves independent local claims open, and Zicherman's limited domestic supplementation does not settle the distinction. Uniformity cannot extend the treaty beyond the class its language identifies. Taking the Second Circuit's nonaccident determination as the premise of this review, without independently approving it, he would leave local proceedings available. He would award no treaty recovery and decides no battery, false imprisonment, loss or local defense.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/el_al_israel_airlines_v_tseng_merits_1999-01-12.md#opinion-topology).

### City of West Covina v. Perkins — 1999-01-13

**Justice Scalia, joined by Justice Thomas, concurring in the judgment.** The concurrence agrees that officials need not give personalized instruction about generally available return law and that the missing warrant number does not sustain the judgment on this record. It withholds assent to the Court's use of civil procedural-due-process decisions as the source of notice requirements when officers execute a criminal search warrant. Mullane's notice setting and Good's civil real-property seizure do not, without further analysis, establish the duties attending execution of a criminal warrant. That source objection does not require approval of secrecy or obstruction, a finding that the Fourth Amendment affirmatively commands a particular inventory, or a holding that it displaces every later due-process protection. The concurrence leaves the constitutional source, timing and detailed content of any separate notice obligation unresolved and joins the limited reversal and remand.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/city_of_west_covina_v_perkins_merits_1999-01-13.md#opinion-topology).

### Marcus v. Iowa Public Television — 1999-01-13

**Justice Stevens, joined by Justices Souter and Ginsburg, dissenting in part.** The dissent joins the committee-based jurisdictional holding and the nonpublic-forum classification, including the permission for reasonable selection and the prohibition of viewpoint discrimination. It maintains the standards objection stated in the Forbes dissent: allocation of a government-provided candidate platform requires sufficiently definite and reviewable restraints on selection. Iowa's fairness policies and illustrative factors describe professional judgment, but Instruction 15 still leaves officials to decide which candidacies interest the audience without adequate operative limits. The dissent accepts the adverse findings about viewpoint motive. Its objection is that sincere discretion and absence of hostility do not cure inadequate standards under Cornelius, Shuttlesworth and Rosenberger. The candidates' assertions about missing information, publicity and comparisons show why meaningful review matters; the dissent does not treat those assertions as findings of equal electoral significance or discrimination.

The dissent would vacate rejection of the committee's common policy claim and remand for consideration under adequately constrained selection standards. It would return the program-guide request for consideration of its connection to permissible equitable relief and any continuing injury the relief could redress, without establishing an independent guide claim or an entitlement to correction. It would order neither automatic ballot-based admission, a universal numerical threshold, a replacement 1996 broadcast, compulsory future programming nor damages. These views remain noncontrolling; they do not change the operative Forbes rule.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/marcus_v_iowa_public_television_merits_1999-01-13.md#opinion-topology).

### Roberts v. Galen of Virginia, Inc. — 1999-01-13



[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/roberts_v_galen_of_virginia_merits_1999-01-13.md#opinion-topology).

### State Street Bank & Trust Co. v. Signature Financial Group, Inc. — 1999-01-14

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** Stone-Zsela agrees that the business-method exclusion and the useful-result sufficiency rule are too broad. Benson and Flook show that a useful computation is not enough; Diehr requires the integrated application to be examined as a whole. Section 112 prevents treating the issued machine claims simply as cancelled methods. He would require the lower court to determine the actual corresponding structure and decide whether the integrated system claims a practical machine application or effectively the accounting mathematics itself. Correcting the appellate test does not, in his view, justify automatically reinstating the District Court's categorical approach. He would vacate and remand that inquiry under Markman without turning every technical finding into a universally de novo issue. He decides neither ultimate validity nor infringement and preserves the separate requirements of novelty, nonobviousness and disclosure.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/state_street_bank_v_signature_financial_group_merits_1999-01-14.md#opinion-topology).

### California Public Employees' Retirement System and Florida State Board of Administration v. Paul Felzen et al. — 1999-01-20

**Justice Stevens, joined by Justices Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, dissenting.** The dissent would recognize a confined appellate role for a shareholder who responds to the Rule 23.1 settlement notice, appears, presents objections, and has those objections heard and rejected in the disposition of the corporate claim. The procedural role, rather than mere ownership, financial exposure, receipt of notice or potential binding effect, supplies status to seek review of that disposition. It does not make the objector a plaintiff controlling prosecution or give a nonappearing shareholder a right to appeal.

Tryforos, which Justice Stevens authored as a circuit judge, supplies the direct distinction between objecting to disposition and undertaking prosecution. Its footnote expressly includes proposed settlements, although the actual objection there concerned dismissal with prejudice and the corporation's lack of service. Marino presents a serious contrary example because its objectors were heard too. The dissent distinguishes their adverse personal interests in another action from the Rule 23.1 shareholder's defined role concerning disposition of the represented corporate claim. Izumi involved an undisturbed intervention denial and a different presented question; it does not foreclose deciding that this recognized role supplies limited status. Johnson offers an equity analogy linking invitation, appearance, objection and appeal, but its adjacent intervention discussion and receivership setting prevent treating it as a universal nonintervention rule. Richards protects independent nonparty rights without itself creating appellate status.

The dissent would reverse only the status dismissal and remand for further appellate proceedings subject to ordinary preservation and appeal requirements. It would not invalidate the settlement, find collusion or inadequate representation, permit new claims, promise success on the objections or establish reviewability or entitlement on every fee contention. It proposes no independent prospective-only ruling, reopening, tolling or automatic late intervention. Its limited representative-objector rule does not control the judgment.

[Published opinion topology](../entering-law/california_public_employees_retirement_system_v_felzen_merits_1999-01-20.md#opinion-topology).

### Lopez v. Monterey County — 1999-01-20

None. The Chief joins Parts I, II and IV and the stated prospective-nonuse and electorate propositions of Part III. He agrees in rejection of the collateral-designation defense and the dependent-tenure disposition without adding grounds.

### AT&T Corp. v. Iowa Utilities Board; Nos. 97-826, 97-829, 97-830, 97-831, 97-1075, 97-1087, 97-1099 and 97-1141; including California respondents in the AT&T, MCI and FCC petitions. — 1999-01-25

**O'Connor, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** Congress authorized federal implementation of §251's express substantive access duties, but §252(d)'s state-pricing assignment does not authorize comprehensive federal replacement of that choice. Rural-exemption and agreement-approval determinations also retain their specific state allocation. National methodology and local rates can sometimes coexist, but that observation cannot itself supply the particular displacement she finds here. She accepts the other rule-specific judgments and joins the lead's other eligible parts. In the California matter, §251(b)(3)'s substantive dialing-parity duty and §251(d)(1)'s implementing instruction support reversal independently of the broader authority rationale. This is no view that States may disregard federal duties, and it decides no TELRIC or actual-rate merits.

**Souter, dissenting in part.** The Act makes unbundled access an instrument of competitive entry rather than a last resort after facilities replication. Increased costs and lost quality affect the requested service; the higher proprietary threshold and nonproprietary alternative give necessity and impairment distinct work. Under Chevron and the contextual delegation analysis of his MCI dissent, the agency reasonably declined a categorical external-source requirement that could demand commercially disabling duplication. He would sustain Rule 51.319 against this statutory attack. He does not demand access to every technically available element. The separate Rule 51.317 presumption still requires review of its burdens and explanation: feasibility may be an additional consideration, and its actual exceptions prevent treating feasibility as its only condition. He would vacate the partial presumption invalidation and remand under the proper authority standard, without unconditional reinstatement or a final validity decision. He joins the rest of the Court's opinion, including the section 208 ripeness disposition.

**Thomas, dissenting in part, joined in the authority and parity grounds by Breyer.** Section 152(b), the interstate context of §201(b), and the Act's particular assignments prevent converting an implementation deadline into general FCC regulatory jurisdiction over every state-assigned task. Louisiana Public Service Commission distinguishes federal substantive obligations from a federal agency's jurisdiction to enforce them by regulation. The new provisions impose national duties but specifically assign pricing and other decisions to state commissions. Detailed federal methodology can replace lawful local policy choices rather than simply establish statutory boundaries. They would retain the identified authority and intraLATA-parity vacaturs, without making substantive parity optional. Both agree with the superior-quality remand under the Court's contrary governing framework and with the section 208 unripe disposition; those agreements do not become general-authority joins.

Thomas alone would also retain invalidation of Rule 51.315(c)–(f). Congress's direction to enable the requesting carrier to combine elements, reinforced by the separate resale route, assigns that affirmative undertaking to the requester. MCI requires the particular allocation to constrain a general implementing grant. The contrary reading stresses that the sentence speaks of enabling rather than exclusive labor, but compensation and feasibility safeguards cannot provide an affirmative duty the statute withholds. Preserving an existing combination requires no new combining work and is therefore different. Necessary accommodation and voluntary new combinations remain available. Breyer does not join this ground and accepts the Court's new-combination remand.

**Breyer, concurring.** He joins the Court's Rule 51.319 criterion holding and adds that the agency has not convincingly explained its treatment of available alternatives, administrative costs and investment incentives. Allentown Mack respects agency expertise when exercised through a reasoned application of its actual lawful standard; it does not supply that explanation here. This additional objection imposes no essential-facilities-only rule, numerical cost threshold or judicial replacement list. It does not withdraw his contrary general-authority position or his conditional remedial agreement on superior-quality and new-combination review.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/att_corp_v_iowa_utilities_board_merits_1999-01-25.md#opinion-topology).

### Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives; Clinton v. Glavin; Nos. 98-404 and 98-564. — 1999-01-25

**Souter's statutory plurality, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens and Ginsburg (Part III-B).** Section 141(a)'s express sampling authorization, together with §141(b)'s use of that tabulation for apportionment, leaves discretion to sample. Section 195 removes apportionment from the command to authorize sampling when the Secretary considers it feasible; it does not prohibit sampling. The exception retains work because the Secretary need not sample for apportionment even when feasible. The retained language and earlier statutory setting are substantial contrary arguments, but do not turn a changed command into an unexpressed prohibition. Section 209 provides review and unsampled preparations, not substantive authorization. The plurality recognizes neither a duty to sample nor immunity for unconstitutional methods. This general statutory construction has four votes.

**Breyer's separate opinion.** Breyer agrees that these methods are lawful, but reads §195 to distinguish replacing enumeration with a sample census from supplementing an actual enumeration effort to correct its omissions. Integrated Coverage Measurement compares and adjusts the initial count. Nonresponse follow-up requires separate analysis because it estimates remaining housing units: direct information from at least ninety percent of a tract's housing units, practical collection difficulty and the Secretary's explained accuracy-and-cost judgment keep this design within permissible supplementation, though it approaches the discretionary boundary. The denominator is housing units, not all persons. Breyer adopts neither a fixed percentage safe harbor nor a cost exception to an express prohibition, and declines Part III-B's broader reading. He joins the constitutional holding. In the House portion, joined by Stevens, he finds a concrete continuing institutional function concerning the apportionment report and lawful representation. Their *Raines* positions distinguish interference with constitutional work from personal legislative disappointment, and *Akins* connects an information entitlement to actual use. Authorization alone is insufficient, but the House's legal continuity permits protection of this function before the membership changes. They would reverse the House judgment on the merits rather than dismiss its action.

**O'Connor's partial concurrence and dissent.** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas read §195's specific retained apportionment restriction to qualify §141(a)'s general methods language. The 1976 change to a feasibility-qualified sampling command expanded required use elsewhere without removing that boundary; §141(b)'s report requirement creates no contrary grant. They accept that an exception from a command is not invariably a prohibition but find the continuous statutory structure decisive here. O'Connor and Kennedy also accept legislative-history corroboration; Scalia and Thomas do not join that ground. All four would affirm the Glavin statutory judgment and injunction as to the challenged apportionment methods, without deciding constitutional validity, banning all statistical work or treating feasibility as an unconditional duty.

**Scalia's partial concurrence and dissent, joined by Kennedy and Thomas.** These Justices additionally find serious constitutional doubt concerning the ordinary historical meaning of enumeration and Congress's long practice, supporting the available restrictive statutory construction. *Franklin*'s residence ruling and *Wisconsin*'s express reservation did not resolve that doubt. This is a supplementary construction argument, not a constitutional holding, an assertion that every estimate is forbidden or a literal census-day physical-presence rule. They join O'Connor's text-and-structure ground, not her legislative-history discussion.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/department_of_commerce_v_house_and_clinton_v_glavin_merits_1999-01-25.md#opinion-topology).

### Boring v. Buncombe County Board of Education, No. 97-1835. — 1999-02-17

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer, would vacate the dismissal and remand. The dissent accepts the school's final authority over instruction but recognizes a limited personal expressive interest against adverse action unrelated to genuine legitimate pedagogy. A teacher need not have a right to insist on a chosen play to object that officials punished an allegedly approved expressive contribution for an impermissible nonpedagogical reason. The dissent would extend Hazelwood's legitimate-pedagogical-relation approach to this claim, informed by Settle's distinction between real educational authority and constitutional immunity. It does not assert that either precedent already decided a teacher's entitlement.

Officials could alter content and take related personnel action reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns. They would not have to satisfy strict scrutiny, prove actual disruption in every case, open a forum, or guarantee Boring's preferred production. But approval, compliance with the requested edits, and punishment for unpopular expression are pleaded; insubordination or a merely conceivable legitimate reason cannot replace an actual supported ground. Waters and Jeffries preserve that genuine-ground and causal distinction. Brewster reinforces attention to the actual employee claim and individualized defenses without treating every internal disagreement as protected.

The dissent would permit ordinary adjudication of protection, actual pedagogical grounds, and cause. Boring would have to show protected conduct was a substantial or motivating factor; defendants could prove by a preponderance that they would have made the same decision without it. Liability, municipal attribution, and individual immunity would remain distinct; immunity notice would be judged as of the 1991–1992 conduct. The proposed remand orders no play, reinstatement, damages, discovery, or trial and permits another supported lawful disposition. These four Justices reject the majority's absence-of-personal-interest conclusion while preserving broad final school control.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/boring_v_buncombe_county_board_of_education_merits_1999-02-17.md#opinion-topology).
