### Reno v. American-Arab Anti-Discrimination Committee; No. 97-1252; Ninth Circuit judgment, 119 F.3d 1367. — 1999-02-24

**Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Kennedy and Thomas.** They join Parts I, II and III-A: the actual transition and named-action rules, subsection (f)'s remedial limits, rejection of the proposed statutory factual-transfer mechanism, and the separation of substantiality, selection merits and interim relief. They reject Part III-B because subsection (g) expressly reaches the action challenged. *Webster* permits choosing among lawful constructions, not removing an expressly covered class merely because access would otherwise fail. *McNary* and *Catholic Social Services* construed narrower statutory objects.

They would vacate the judgment sustaining original selection-claim jurisdiction and set aside both existing injunctions. The Six's refusal-to-dissolve posture cannot insulate an injunction from a newly operative jurisdictional withdrawal, and the Two's initial order needs jurisdiction independently. This does not declare the preliminary findings false or direct removal.

They would nevertheless retain the already presented substantial contention that applying the review exclusion here is unconstitutional and therefore ineffective. Section 1331 permits that limited present inquiry in this case. Scalia and Thomas's *Thunder Basin* formulation and the three Justices' *Kolster* reservations support confronting the actual exclusion question instead of certifying a promised future remedy. Kennedy joins this confined rule, which does not announce a universal formula for every asserted exclusion. Jurisdiction to decide exclusion's constitutional effectiveness is distinct from jurisdiction over the underlying selection merits.

The remand would examine the missing factual route, timing, actual injury and asserted need for a forum, together with individual continuing controversy. An incidental interim request could be considered only upon an established source and the required equitable showing; the existing orders do not automatically convert into such relief. They decide neither that exclusion is constitutional nor that every alien must await a final order. They would not dismiss the entire action while this identified question remains unanswered.

**Stone-Zsela, dissenting.** He would vacate the injunctions and remand with instructions to dismiss the collateral action for want of jurisdiction. Section 1252(g) applies to the pending action and reaches its effort to restrain commencement and adjudication. It remains confined to the three named executive acts; detention, evidence gathering, adjudicative procedure and legal errors do not fall within it merely because they occur during removal. Applying that channel changes forum and timing rather than imposing new liability.

In his assessment, this record establishes neither permanent loss of meaningful review nor an independently irremediable injury requiring immediate freestanding adjudication. The constitutional theory can be preserved while the statutory proceedings continue. He does not adopt an identified section 2347 mechanism, pronounce an unfiled habeas proceeding adequate or erase the lower courts' preliminary findings. His conclusion is that those materials do not establish the constitutional necessity of this present collateral forum. He therefore also rejects the three-Justice proposal to retain the separate exclusion challenge now. Whether the objection may later be heard on final-order review or through another constitutionally indispensable channel on materially different facts remains open. He reaches no retaliation merits, approves no elimination of every adequate review route and adds no removal direction.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/reno_v_american_arab_anti_discrimination_committee_merits_1999-02-24.md#opinion-topology).

### Minns v. United States; No. 98-903; OT1998. — 1999-02-25

**Thomas's Part II, joined by O'Connor, Kennedy and Breyer,** concludes that the pleaded safety and warning duties remain dependent on selecting military-preparation policy. It accepts that prescribed duties and ordinary nonpolicy negligence are different, but finds no separable claim in these allegations. The four do not decide Feres through that statutory conclusion.

**O'Connor, joined by Stone-Zsela, concurs in the judgment.** Feres and Stencel protect the service relationship from claims whose necessary wrongful conduct is the military preparation of the fathers. Changing the person in whom the alleged harm manifests does not remove that required inquiry. Their conclusion does not deny the children's interests or excuse misconduct; it concerns the available damages waiver. A genuinely distinct civilian duty, nonservice negligence, express statute or administrative compensation requires its own analysis.

**Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Scalia and Souter, concurs in part and dissents in part.** Children's own bodily injuries are not automatically derivative losses merely because a parent's military treatment appears in the causal chain. Feres does not itself answer every such civilian claim. Berkovitz and Gaubert protect actual policy choices, but the lower court should examine the distinct execution allegations instead of equating military activity with policy. The proposed remand would establish no state tort, violated directive, discovery entitlement or right to recovery; other exceptions would remain available.

**Scalia also writes separately.** His Gaubert concurrence ties protected policy discretion to the responsibilities entrusted to the actor. The policy link established for bank supervision there cannot simply be attributed to every unidentified person storing or administering a substance here. He joins the limited remand without proposing a planning/operations rule or compulsory testimony about subjective deliberations.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/minns_v_united_states_merits_1999-02-25.md#opinion-topology).

### Holloway v. United States; No. 97-7164; OT1998. — 1999-03-02

**Scalia dissents.** In ordinary usage, an unqualified intent to cause a result does not include merely planning to cause it upon resistance the actor hopes will never occur. He does not say every conceivable contingency defeats intent: remote implicit contingencies, or a likely condition the actor hopes will occur, present different ordinary-language uses. The divided state authorities do not establish a federal term of art overriding the enacted words. Congress's deterrent objective cannot erase the means it chose; any remaining criminal ambiguity favors the defendant even where the conduct is otherwise blameworthy. He would reverse the affected carjacking affirmances and return the dependent firearm judgments for lawful further proceedings, without ordering acquittal or disturbing unrelated counts.

**Thomas dissents separately.** Congress did not expressly define intent to include the asserted conditional purpose, and the cited decisions and commentary do not establish a sufficiently settled legal convention to presume that specialized meaning. Selected state statutes and the Model Penal Code are interpretive evidence, not enacted federal law. He reaches the same proposed disposition without adopting Scalia's entire linguistic classification or examples. His position likewise resolves no unpresented sufficiency, harmless-error or retrial issue.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/holloway_v_united_states_merits_1999-03-02.md#opinion-topology).

### American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan; No. 97-2000; OT1998. — 1999-03-03

**Ginsburg concurs in Part II and in the judgment.** Rejecting constant payment of every medical bill pending the reasonable-and-necessary determination is sufficient; she does not reach private attribution. Claims for medical benefits still require fair adjudication. She agrees with Stevens that the original absence of adequate notice and employee input was deficient and that the revised features answer that particular objection, without deciding every delay, lost-care or prior-award claim.

**Breyer, joined by Souter, concurs.** They join the private-attribution holding and the confined rejection of the facial payment demand. Prior payments may create a reasonable expectation of continuation in an individual case. The present ruling neither finds nor forecloses that property interest and does not assume that every interruption is brief or reparable with money. Their qualification is not an unconditional right to every disputed bill.

**Stevens concurs in part and dissents in part.** The substantive claim to reasonable and necessary care is property, and government must determine it fairly. The original procedure did not adequately warn of suspension or permit meaningful employee input. He would retain the notice and statement protections imposed below and reject additional categorical advance payment because withholding pending a fair determination is not inherently unfair. He declines the Court's attribution and entitlement rationales, does not declare the carriers governmental, and would leave the remaining actual claims for proper resolution. He dissents from the additional attribution adjudication while agreeing that further automatic payment requirements cannot stand under the revised procedure.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/american_manufacturers_v_sullivan_merits_1999-03-03.md#opinion-topology).

### Cedar Rapids Community School District v. Garret F.; No. 96-1793; OT1998. — 1999-03-03

**Thomas, joined by Kennedy, dissents.** Intensive continuing respiratory and bodily care can be medical by nature even if supplied by a nurse. The regulation's inclusion of physician diagnostic services does not, in their view, define every excluded service. They would disapprove Tatro's contrary general provider-based construction to that extent rather than treat it as never having addressed the question.

Their statutory reading also gives insufficiently clear notice of this continuing-care obligation as a funding condition. Auer cannot enlarge what they understand Congress to have authorized. They do not adopt an open-ended local cost test, find this district unable to pay, deny Garret's educational benefit or treat the Anderson letter as an express agency adoption of the district's proposal.

The ALJ found the costs manageable under an alternative burden inquiry and separately relied on Iowa law. The dissenters' categorical federal construction would make affordability insufficient for federal coverage, but it would not eliminate that independent state ground. They would remand for its resolution, including the asserted higher-state-standard route through IDEA and the district's contention that Iowa law tracks federal law. They would not order final dismissal or automatically extinguish reimbursement.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/cedar_rapids_v_garret_f_merits_1999-03-03.md#opinion-topology).

### Federal Republic of Germany, Chrobog and Rudolph v. United States and Hull; No. 127, Original; A-736. — 1999-03-03

**Stone-Zsela, Part II.** The Chief would decline this nonexclusive original action. He recognizes the actual diplomats' Article III and §1251(b)(1) category for considering leave and Hull's alleged concrete connection, but finds no established domestic command enforceable by the requested remedy. Article 94 addresses the member state's undertaking to the ICJ; the Optional Protocol identifies the international tribunal. Neither specifies this domestic action or makes the provisional measure a judicial stay binding the Governor. Article VI does not itself answer enforceability through this remedy, and the ICJ's reference to measures within national power does not enlarge that power. The United States has not consented to this distinct domestic suit. These grounds concern this pleading and remedy, leave the international obligation unresolved and do not foreclose a different properly authorized claim.

**Scalia, additional concurrence.** O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas join his conclusion that the Court can assess the present interim showing without an additional authority-preserving interval. The loss of the requested remedy does not by itself establish that extra protection is necessary to determine power. Scalia alone also requires a sufficiently clear basis for the affirmative original injunction; his stronger formulation commands no other join and creates no additional Court holding.

**Stevens, dissent, joined by Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer.** These Justices would restrain Hull and those actually acting under her authority until 5 p.m. Eastern Standard Time on March 5, 1999 or an earlier Court order, with earlier vacation upon determination that the relevant jurisdiction or injunctive power is absent. *Shipp* and *United Mine Workers* permit necessary preservation during a substantial inquiry into authority; *Pennsylvania Bureau* keeps §1651 auxiliary. Assuming §2283 applies, they find its necessary-in-aid exception satisfied by this particular new preservation duty, real diplomatic-party original action, concretely connected officer and finite inquiry. They do not rely on short duration or gravity alone, adopt automatic domestic treaty enforcement, or grant protection through the ICJ merits. Their proposed interval requires no new filing or hearing and carries no automatic extension.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/federal_republic_of_germany_original_leave_and_interim_order_1999-03-03.md#opinion-topology).

### National Federation of Federal Employees, Local 1309 v. Department of the Interior / Federal Labor Relations Authority v. Department of the Interior; Nos. 97-1184 and 97-1243. — 1999-03-03

**O'Connor, dissenting, Part I, joined by Scalia and Thomas.** The agreement-directed duty in §7114, the separately structured continuing obligations and §7101's efficiency instruction together supply a statutory boundary. In their view, the broad definition of bargaining and the required frequency of meetings regulate negotiations already required; they do not authorize a comprehensive continuing union-initiated regime. The statute's public impasse system matters because bargaining may lead, after the statutory conditions are satisfied, to a binding lawful resolution. They do not assume that this particular clause will inevitably be imposed.

They accept that the absence of an entitlement ordinarily differs from a prohibition on negotiation. This general advance clause nevertheless recreates the compulsory regime they find inconsistent with the statutory structure and §7117(a). Its reference to negotiable uncovered subjects does not cure that temporal objection. They would affirm the refusal of enforcement while reserving genuinely narrower clauses and voluntary negotiations on particular subjects. They do not treat every statutory omission as a matter specifically settled under §7103(a)(14)(C), and do not revive the dismissed allegations against Interior.

**O'Connor, Part II, alone.** She adds that the Authority's court-induced reversal, continued assumption of a statutory mandate and limited independent explanation do not establish a fully reasoned administrative choice deserving acceptance. *Good Samaritan* permits diminished weight for a changed interpretation but does not automatically disqualify a considered permissible one; her objection concerns the actual reasons and asserted legal setting, not change alone. She would examine the text and structure without assuming that compliance with an earlier judicial interpretation supplies the agency's independent justification. Her statutory ground remains sufficient, and she does not announce a general displacement of *Chevron*. Scalia and Thomas do not join this additional ground.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/nffe_and_flra_v_interior_merits_1999-03-03.md#opinion-topology).

### Stewart v. LaGrand; No. 98-1412; distinct State application A-735. — 1999-03-03

No separate writing accompanies the petition order.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/stewart_v_lagrand_certiorari_1999-03-03.md#opinion-topology).

### Stewart v. LaGrand — application and summary merits — 1999-03-03

**Kennedy, joined by O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas.** Kennedy adopts both Court grounds as his Circuit Justice explanation. His concurrence also concludes that, even assuming an available procedural route, the renewed opportunity to avoid the particular gas method through an alternative unchallenged here defeats continued support for this restraint. *Gomez* permits a distinct equitable assessment; *Lonchar* preserves that inquiry while requiring genuinely necessary process. The earlier election prerequisite explains the renewed timing, so this concurrence finds neither universal delay nor abusive motive. This additional interim rationale has four supporters and does not independently control.

**Stevens, joined by Stone-Zsela.** The dissent would deny immediate vacatur and retain the existing gas-only protection on its actual terms while the serious consent and constitutional-limit question receives full briefing and argument. The earlier appellate court made selection a prerequisite to considering the gas claim. That sequence makes summary treatment of selection as surrender of the objection especially problematic. The dissent accepts that unfavorable precedent or later election-based ripeness alone does not establish cause, but would not remove protection on that correction before proper claim-specific consideration. It adopts neither a universal nonwaivability rule nor a categorical judgment about gas, and creates no general execution stay.

**Stone-Zsela's additional signed statement.** Choosing among statutory methods does not, without more, establish knowledgeable relinquishment of an Eighth Amendment objection to the selected method. The record also does not establish an adequate, independent and unexcused state bar on the later gas-related ineffective-assistance claim, including any applicable exception and its relationship to the earlier representation-choice proceedings. He therefore denies the application and opposes summary reversal, joining Stevens's dissent and seeking proper consideration on a developed record with the existing gas-only protection retained on its actual terms. This reasoning supplies neither a categorical method holding nor automatic excuse from an actually established default.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/stewart_v_lagrand_application_and_summary_merits_1999-03-03.md#opinion-topology).

### Gable v. Patton / Patton v. Gable — threshold decision — 1999-03-04

**Stevens, dissenting, joined by Stone-Zsela, Ginsburg and Breyer.** The dissent would reach both granted questions and affirm the Sixth Circuit on each. Gable is an identified former candidate who says these same restrictions deter his return. His nonfiling is consistent with that asserted injury. Requiring him to campaign under the challenged terms would discount the very deterrence alleged. The dissent finds his particular showing sufficient without finding a 1999 filing or granting every former candidate an indefinite right to litigate. Stevens's *Lujan* concurrence and the published standing disagreement in *Commerce / Glavin* support attention to concrete personal activity across changing electoral circumstances.

On financing, the dissent would uphold the challenged voluntary program and responsive trigger on this record. The nonparticipant retains his own money and freedom to spend; a comparative electoral disadvantage is not itself a coercive expenditure cap. Further public funds depend on the participant's additional qualifying fundraising, matched two for one after the required certification that the nonparticipant received contributions or made expenditures above the ceiling. They are not an automatic dollar-for-dollar payment matching the opponent's speech. The dissent's position follows the *Day* financing dissent's distinction between enlarging competing advocacy and restraining a speaker, while preserving challenges to genuinely coercive or punitive operation. It recognizes no numerical safe harbor, categorical subsidy immunity or unlimited public-payment obligation.

On personal funds, the dissent would affirm invalidation of the twenty-eight-day prohibition only insofar as it bars a nonparticipant from adding personal funds to finance further advocacy. Previously deposited money remains spendable, but that opportunity does not replace a later decision to finance responsive speech. *Buckley*, *Kruse* and *Suster* protect the candidate's own expression; a candidate's personal resources are not conventional donor contributions. Administration, notice and an opponent's opportunity to answer do not justify this complete cutoff. The dissent would preserve the distinction between own funds and ordinary third-party contributions, leaving the ungranted donor, sponsor-identification and slating questions open. It would not prescribe a replacement reporting code or treat every statutory branch as inseverable. These are dissenting merits views; none controls the remand.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/gable_v_patton_and_patton_v_gable_threshold_1999-03-04.md#opinion-topology).

### Jackson v. Benson; No. 98-376; OT1998. — 1999-03-16

**Stone-Zsela concurs in Part I and dissents from Part II.** He would reverse the federal approval and require relief preventing public payment for religious-school tuition or integrated devotional and inseparably sectarian instruction, including payment through checks, vouchers, tax credits, reimbursements, provider-redemption instruments or another third-party route. His objection is to the function purchased with restricted public value, not the religious identity of the school. Neither the parent's selection nor the instrument's form makes the purchased religious formation privately financed.

He distinguishes Agostini's publicly controlled secular service and Rosenberger's independently edited publication benefit. He preserves genuine neutral forums, genuinely severable publicly controlled secular services, and the distinct arrangements in Witters and Mueller. Actual operative payment terms, instruction and severability determine the reach of relief. He would direct application of state severability law to the secular-school portion, permitting preservation of genuinely severable publicly controlled secular services; ungranted state-law judgments remain untouched. His required reversal and payment direction are not the Court's more limited vacatur.

**Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, dissents.** The combined program facts establish a genuine individual educational benefit: faith-neutral child eligibility, real family selection among supported public and private choices, independently operated schools and payment bounded by enrollment and educational cost. In their view, cost verification defines the amount of the child's benefit rather than making the chosen curriculum a governmental religious undertaking. They acknowledge the integrated instruction and possibility of payment beyond tuition; they do not treat a parent signature or neutrality alone as conclusive.

Witters shows that a genuine individual benefit may support privately chosen religious training. Rosenberger, Agostini and the private-access decisions preserve the difference between governmental activity and independently directed religious use. The dissenters would affirm this program while leaving actual governmental direction, religious preference, sham choice or other independently prohibited administration for a properly supported claim. They recognize no universal voucher rule, constitutional duty to subsidize or right to compel a school to participate. If corrective relief is entered, it must remain confined; that conditional limit does not join the Court's financing premise or vacatur.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/jackson_v_benson_merits_1999-03-16.md#opinion-topology).

### National Committee of the Reform Party of the United States of America v. Democratic National Committee; Ninth Circuit No. 98-15443; 168 F.3d 360. — 1999-03-17

Stone-Zsela joins Parts I-III and the judgment. He agrees that the presented subsidy and contribution-gap theory supplies no constitutional duty of parity and that the affiliation claim lacks a redressable connection to the statutory maximum. He does not join Part IV. The separate certification judgments, like the damages judgments, lie outside the specified grant; he would leave them undisturbed without the additional certification discussion. He supplies no alternative holding on their merits.

[Complete opinion topology](../entering-law/reform_party_v_democratic_national_committee_merits_1999-03-17.md#opinion-topology).

### Central State University v. American Association of University Professors, Central State University Chapter; No. 98-1071. — 1999-03-22

Stevens dissents from the Court's summary treatment. He would deny review or, after the grant, permit plenary consideration of the occupational distinction and the state court's rationale. His Beach and Nordlinger positions require attention to the actual comparison between faculty and other workers; a general management objective should not obscure that inquiry. He acknowledges the possible force of the teaching-management connection and does not decide that Ohio's exclusion ultimately fails federal equal protection. His objection does not adopt an empirical-proof requirement or establish a separate academic-freedom holding.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/central_state_university_v_aaup_summary_merits_1999-03-22.md).

### Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael; No. 97-1709; OT1998. — 1999-03-23

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela joins Parts I–II and Part III-A's permissible-exclusion proposition and unsupported-inference ground, and concurs in reversal, but dissents from Part III-B's operative remand. Rule 702 addresses the knowledge offered rather than the expert's professional label. Carlson's experience was relevant, but he did not reliably explain how his inspection distinguished defect from abuse or why the observations supported the asserted cause; the District Court was entitled to reject that unsupported analytical bridge. Stone-Zsela nevertheless would direct the Eleventh Circuit to review the reliability ruling for abuse of discretion under the complete Rule 702 framework and reinstate summary judgment if no admissible evidence supports defect and causation. His position preserves flexible reliability screening without mandatory peer review or testing in every technical field, displacing the jury on reliable disputed evidence, or excluding a well-supported conclusion merely because the judge disagrees with it.

Justice Stevens joins Parts I–II but dissents from Part III and the judgment. The common Rule 702 obligation defeats the Eleventh Circuit's categorical exemption, but that court should first apply the correct framework to the complete technical proffer. As in his Joiner position, agreement on appellate review and inferential fit does not settle the actual exercise of discretion. He would vacate and remand for appellate application, without ordering admission, reopening every foundation submission or requiring a new hearing.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/kumho_tire_v_carmichael_merits_1999-03-23.md).

### Jones v. United States; No. 97-6203; OT1998. — 1999-03-24

**Souter, joined by Stevens, Scalia and Ginsburg.** These four join the Court's sufficient statutory ground and also conclude that a fact about the commission or consequences of the charged offense, indispensable to a greater statutory maximum than the charged elements and verdict otherwise allow, performs the constitutional function of an element. The federal charging, jury and reasonable-doubt safeguards follow, subject to valid admission and waiver rules; those rules do not repair the omitted charge by implication. Their Monge position treated an additional, previously unestablished personal-conduct qualification as an element for the finality question. They now apply that functional reasoning to injury in the present offense, with Gaudin supplying jury allocation. This is their constitutional conclusion here, not a claim that Monge already decided the jury question. Ordinary factfinding within existing conviction-authorized punishment remains distinct. The concurrence leaves bare prior-conviction status, unchanged-maximum minima, general Guidelines classification and capital sentencing unresolved. Four Justices adopt this independent constitutional ground; it is not the Court's holding.

**Stevens, joined by Scalia in Part I.** Their broader position protects facts that change the legislatively prescribed punishment range, including facts increasing the minimum, and recognizes no categorical exclusion merely because the fact is a prior conviction. Stevens relies on Winship's proof requirement, Mullaney's protection against shifting the burden on a fact determining punishment, his objections to Patterson's treatment of offense definition, and Duncan's protection of jury adjudication. Scalia agrees that the legislature cannot avoid the protection by naming a range-determining fact a sentencing consideration. His narrower Monge element-function ground supports that conclusion without having already established this universal range principle. Neither ordinary relevant-conduct sentencing under Witte and Watts nor an existing conviction's validity is the same question as whether a new fact legally authorizes a different punishment range. A charged prior-status element may be established by adequate admission or other lawful proof without relitigating the conviction's validity; Custis's collateral-review limits remain. These positions do not decide a mandatory-minimum application or overrule McMillan here. In Part II, Stevens alone also places facts necessary for death eligibility within his constitutional principle and favors reconsideration of contrary Walton reasoning in an appropriate case. No capital issue is decided, and Scalia does not join that recommendation.

**Stone-Zsela.** The Chief Justice joins the statutory construction and remedy as sufficient to decide this case. He maintains that a fact increasing authorized punishment must receive the jury protection owed an element, but does not make that broader position an additional constitutional ground of this disposition or join Souter's independent constitutional concurrence. Almendarez-Torres supplies a statutory analogue, not an already controlling universal constitutional rule. His maintained position preserves his view while the Court's decision rests on the enacted aggravated offenses. It supplies no decision of the separate questions concerning prior status, unchanged-maximum minima or capital sentencing.

**Kennedy, joined by O'Connor and Breyer.** The dissent reads the opening firearm-taking paragraph as a complete offense and the dependent injury and death clauses as graded punishment for its consequences. The sharp increases and the traditional use of injury to grade robbery support the Court's reading, but do not overcome that offense-first structure in the dissent's view. Patterson preserves a bounded legislative role in defining an offense; Gaudin protects the jury's determination once an actual element is identified. The dissent directly defends treating injury during this completed offense as a punishment condition, acknowledging that present victim injury differs from recidivist character. Monge accepted both an unproved personal-conduct qualification and a higher ceiling without treating those features as conclusive of offense status, while leaving the independent jury question open. The dissent therefore does not claim that Monge already resolved this claim or that the qualification was previously proved. Almendarez-Torres's statutory reentry holding remains binding but supplies no general constitutional rule either permitting or forbidding this allocation. McMillan's unchanged-ceiling setting and Witte's and Watts's within-authority sentencing do not themselves resolve the increased maximum. The dissent would sustain the challenged injury-based sentencing authority against this classification objection, without adopting unlimited legislative relabeling, resolving unrelated sentencing errors or directing reinstatement of an earlier sentence.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/jones_v_united_states_merits_1999-03-24.md).

### Minnesota v. Mille Lacs Band of Chippewa Indians; No. 97-1337. — 1999-03-24

The common dissent of Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas would find the rights extinguished on three independent grounds. The treaty's revocation authority gives the revocation command independent operation even if removal was unauthorized. The 1855 comprehensive cession, in their view, reaches the retained use interest; negotiation silence does not overcome that text. Race Horse's temporary-right analysis also applies to a privilege conditioned on presidential pleasure. They reject the Court's removal-purpose, land-purchase-context and conservation-coexistence answers, while confining their 1855 ground to the agreement's parties and rights and declining to nullify every tribal right at statehood.

Thomas separately questions whether survival alone establishes that Minnesota's regulatory authority is limited to conservation necessity. He considers the treaty's pleasure-qualified privilege distinct from the perpetual usual-and-accustomed-place language in other fishing cases and would require a treaty-specific examination of regulatory scope. His objection decides no particular state regulation and receives no additional join.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/minnesota_v_mille_lacs_band_merits_1999-03-24.md).

### Brower v. Washington; No. 98-1510; judgment below Brower v. State, 137 Wash. 2d 44, 969 P.2d 42. — 1999-03-25

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/brower_v_washington_merits_1999-03-25.md).

### United States v. Rodriguez-Moreno, No. 97-1139. — 1999-03-30

Justice Scalia, joined by Justice Stevens, would affirm. Section 924(c) punishes firearm use or carriage occurring during and in relation to the predicate crime. Both acts are elements, and the firearm offense may continue through multiple districts when those acts coincide there. But the unarmed portion of a kidnapping does not extend that distinct firearm offense to every place the victim was held. Cabrales's attention to the particular charged conduct reinforces that limit. The continuing nature of kidnapping therefore does not answer where the combined firearm offense occurred. The dissent does not adopt a rule identifying conduct from statutory verbs alone, disregard the predicate element or resolve how many violations may arise from one predicate. Its venue vacatur would not be an acquittal or a direction concerning a new prosecution.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/united_states_v_rodriguez_moreno_merits_1999-03-30.md).

### Conn v. Gabbert, No. 97-1802; judgment below, 131 F.3d 793. — 1999-04-05

Justice Stevens concurs in part. He joins the occupational-merits holding and reversal because the reported record does not establish deprivation of Gabbert's own professional liberty. He does not join the additional case-specific classification of the search objection. The possible existence of Fourth Amendment protection cannot extinguish an otherwise established, independent due-process violation: Soldal and James Daniel Good preserve that distinction. The absent evidence of professional effects and the separate absence of substantial client prejudice explain his conclusion on this record; they are not universal financial-loss or permanent-exclusion requirements. He finds no occupational violation here and does not seek a ruling on the separate search claim, the client's rights or qualified immunity.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/conn_v_gabbert_merits_1999-04-05.md).

### Mitchell v. United States; No. 97-7541; OT1998. — 1999-04-05

**Scalia, joined by O'Connor and Thomas.** The three join Part I because the plea did not waive privilege on reserved quantity, but distinguish the right to refuse compelled testimony from an ordinary evidentiary inference drawn alongside credited evidence. They accept Griffin and Carter at their established guilt-phase scope and decline the sentencing extension. Baxter and Woodard illustrate the distinction between inference, compelled answers and an automatic penalty for invocation; sentencing's different evidentiary rules, recognized in McMillan, remain relevant. They dispute the majority's separation of offense circumstances from other sentencing information. Their position permits neither compulsory testimony, treating the plea as an admission of reserved quantity, an automatic privilege penalty nor proof by silence alone. Scalia retains his broader Jones penalty-range position without deciding a new quantity-classification claim here. They find no error and therefore do not reach harmlessness as a ground for affirmance. Conditional acceptance of lawful relief under the Court's contrary rule adds no vote for reversal.

**Thomas.** Thomas joins Scalia's opinion but separately questions the constitutional foundations of Griffin and Carter: an ordinary inference does not itself compel testimony, and a statutory no-comment policy does not necessarily establish a constitutional prohibition. He would consider reconsidering those decisions in an appropriate case. He casts no vote to overrule them here, and no other Justice joins this prospective recommendation. Scalia instead preserves their established operation.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/mitchell_v_united_states_merits_1999-04-05.md).

### Murphy Brothers, Inc. v. Michetti Pipe Stringing, Inc., No. 97-1909; judgment below, 125 F.3d 1396. — 1999-04-05

Justice Thomas, joined by Justice Scalia, would affirm the appellate remand direction. The first statutory branch expressly makes receipt of the filed initial pleading the triggering event and permits receipt otherwise than by service. Formal service is a means of receipt under that language, not an additional condition. The second branch expressly requires summons service when the pleading has been filed and need not be served, and the shorter period remains controlling. General service principles cannot add the second branch's process condition to the first. Congress may require an election of a federal forum before state process imposes a duty to answer; an expired removal period neither authorizes default without service nor establishes personal jurisdiction. Because Murphy removed more than thirty days after receipt of the fax, the dissent finds the removal untimely. It adopts no waiver, bad-faith or equitable-extension finding.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/murphy_brothers_v_michetti_pipe_stringing_merits_1999-04-05.md).

### Wyoming v. Houghton; No. 98-184. — 1999-04-05

Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg would qualify Labron for a closed personal effect known before intrusion to belong to a passenger when vehicle probable cause derives from someone else. Location and capacity alone would not authorize the search. They would require particularized facts connecting the effect or the passenger to the object sought, establishing probable cause reaching the effect, or another independently applicable search ground. Passenger-related facts can supply that connection; the evidence need not originate from the effect itself. Independently supported probable cause reaching the passenger's effect, consent and other established exceptions remain available.

Wellons supplies the distinct privacy interest, and Di Re supports refusing to transfer suspicion merely from the car to the passenger; neither already establishes their proposed exception. They acknowledge Ross, Acevedo and Labron as the principal contrary authority. A uniform container rule, in their view, gives insufficient protection to an identified person's private effects. They answer the concealment and administrability concerns by requiring concrete indicators of transfer, shared ownership or common concealment rather than presuming those circumstances from companionship. The qualification preserves ordinary vehicle compartments, unclaimed or shared containers and independently justified searches. It creates no blanket passenger immunity, universal luggage-warrant rule or separate automobile-exigency test.

Young's syringe and admission supplied vehicle probable cause but no reported fact tied Houghton's effect or her participation to the drugs before entry. Her claimed lack of identification did not by itself create narcotics probable cause; the false identity was discovered through the intrusion requiring justification. The later pouch, track marks and admission about the black container likewise cannot justify the first search retrospectively. All four would adopt the stated limited modification and affirm the reviewed suppression judgment. They make no independent-source finding and propose no acquittal or unqualified disposition of every later intrusion.

[Authority and public scope](../entering-law/wyoming_v_houghton_merits_1999-04-05.md).

## Prior-term published positions retained for continuity

Earlier published positions retain their stated noncontrolling scope and must be read from the relevant prior-term public renders when material to a case; Standing State carries none. The first firearms sequence requires the date-eligible public Rybar writings and applicable prior arms and Commerce Clause law.

In Rybar (November 24, 1997), Stone-Zsela's published dissent would affirm the possession convictions based on the restriction's fit to the distinctive sustained-fire risk and expansion of civilian stocks of the identified completed automatic weapons. His application is noncontrolling, adopts no categorical dangerous- or military-arms exclusion, and does not equate those weapons with ordinary semiautomatics. It supplies no result for receivers, conversion components, or accessories. The [published entry](../../OT1997/output/OT_1997CHUNK1.md#united-states-v-rybar-third-circuit-no-95-3185) preserves the full grounds and limits. The Court's separate weapon-and-restriction inquiry and the remand remain controlling; Commerce authority does not decide the arms claim.

In Home Builders (June 8, 1998), O'Connor's published dissent, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, insists on a connection fitted to the enacted regulated class while accepting Hale's demonstrated anti-evasion route. Stone-Zsela joins the Court's sufficient economic-conversion ground; his additional substantial-interstate-externality framework is a noncontrolling proposal. Their full distinct grounds and qualifications appear in the [published entry](../../OT1997/output/OT_1997CHUNK10.md#national-association-of-home-builders-v-babbitt-dc-circuit-no-96-5354). These positions do not replace the published controlling rules or determine a later vote.

Later current-term decisions enter only when actually completed and effective. No private position, provisional commitment, or historical comparator is incorporated here.


## Source limits

The opening law cutoff remains September 28, 1998. Current-term Records take effect only on their stated dates. Each linked public copy carries its particular source limits; record research gaps establish no litigant default. Source cutoffs are event-specific and do not certify later historical developments. No private position, provisional commitment, comparator or audit material is included.
